--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot → robots should MOVE before switch
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should STOP after switch
- Switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up (color 2 = green in camera terms? Let me check: clr values are {1,2,3,4,5,6}, and C=7 looks for colour with --l)

Wait, re-reading: the wall lights up GREEN. The robot can perceive colored objects with C=7 (perceives a given colour). Green would be color 2 (typical mapping: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan).

**Optimal behavior:**
- Before switch: MOVE (explore, repulse, attract) → minimize stationary robots
- After switch: STOP → minimize moving robots
- Trigger: when green light is perceived (C=7 with color 2), transition from moving to stopped

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: Repulsion (moving), transitions based on neighbor counts and gray floor
- State 1: Stop, transitions back based on neighbors and gray floor
- Score 11656.20 - decent but can be improved

The champion doesn't use the GREEN color perception! That's the key signal. Let me design machines that:
1. Start moving (exploration/repulsion)
2. Switch to Stop when green is seen (C=7, l=2)

**Design strategy:**
- 2-state: Move → Stop (triggered by seeing green, color 2)
- 3-state: variations with attraction/exploration
- Keep champion alive

For the color condition: C=7 requires --l and --p. Green = color 2.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion** (exact copy)
2. **Exploitation**: Small tweaks to champion
3. **Exploration**: Green-signal aware machines

FSM designs:
- State 0: Exploration/Repulsion (moving before switch)
- State 1: Stop (after switch, triggered by green perception)

Key insight: transition from moving to stop should fire when green is seen (C=7, l=2, high probability). Also need a way back from stop to moving if signal is lost? No - once stopped, stay stopped.

Actually from Stop state, we need some transitions (required). We can have low-probability random transitions back to moving, but that would hurt. Better to have transitions that are very unlikely to fire (very low p).

Let me build the machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:**
The champion (score 6344.32) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor (green, color 2) → moves toward the green wall (moving before switch!)
- State 1: Stop → stands still
- Transition 0→1: C=7 (perceives green) with p=0.9 → when green is seen, go to Stop
- Transition 0→1: C=5 (random) with p=0.003 → tiny random chance to stop early
- Transition 1→0: C=5 (random) with p=0.001 → tiny chance to go back to moving

This is clever! Before the switch: robots move (GoToColor green - but green isn't visible yet, so they might wander). After switch: green appears, they perceive it (C=7), and transition to Stop with p=0.9.

Wait - GoToColor with color 2 (green): before the switch, no green wall exists, so the robot might just spin or wander. After the switch, green appears and robots move toward it... but then C=7 fires and stops them. 

**Issues to address:**
1. Before switch: robots should move MORE reliably (GoToColor green when no green = undefined behavior, maybe exploration is better)
2. The p=0.9 for seeing green → stop is good but maybe higher would help
3. The tiny p=0.003 random stop is risky before switch

**Improvements to try:**
- State 0: Exploration (moves reliably before switch) → Stop when green seen
- State 0: GoToColor green → Stop when green seen (champion topology)
- Try p=1.0 for green perception → immediate stop
- Add neighbor-based transitions
- 3-state machines with exploration → GoToColor → Stop

The key insight: when green appears, we want ALL robots to stop ASAP. So p=1.0 for C=7 transition to Stop is ideal.

Also: the random p=0.003 in the champion means ~0.3% per step chance of stopping early. Over 400-600 steps, that's significant. Removing it or lowering it might help.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2-5. Exploitation: tweak probabilities, add more transitions
6-14. Exploration: different base behaviors, 3-state machines

Key experiments:
- p=1.0 instead of 0.9 for green detection
- Remove or lower random stop (p=0.001 or 0.0005)
- Use Exploration instead of GoToColor as base movement
- Use Repulsion as base movement (keeps robots spread, all moving)
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop

# ROUND 4/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 5838.88):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green=2) → moves toward green wall (moves before switch, moves toward green after switch appears)
- Transition 0→1: C=7 (sees green) p=0.9 → when green appears, 90% chance per step to stop
- Transition 0→1: C=5 (random) p=0.001 → very small chance to stop randomly
- State 1: Stop
- Transition 1→0: C=5 (random) p=0.0005 → tiny chance to resume moving

This is very good. The key mechanism: before switch, robots move (no green visible). After switch, green wall appears, robots see it and stop with p=0.9 per step.

**Problems to address:**
1. p=0.9 means on average 1/(0.9) ≈ 1.1 steps to stop once green is seen - but not all robots may see the green wall at once (range-and-bearing only sees other robots, but camera can see colored objects at distance). Actually, GoToColor moves toward green, which helps robots see it faster after switch.
2. The random p=0.001 stop before switch costs ~0.001×500×20 = 10 robot-steps. Very small.
3. The p=0.0005 restart from Stop is ~0.0005×700×20 = 7 robot-steps. Very small.

**Key improvements to explore:**
- p=1.0 for green detection (immediate stop, no delay)
- Lower random stop probability even more
- What if robots use Exploration before switch (more reliable movement than GoToColor when no green)?
- What if state 0 is Exploration and we add a transition to GoToColor state, then Stop?
- 3-state: Explore → (on green) GoToColor → (on green) Stop

Wait - with p=1.0, every step a robot sees green it transitions. That should be better than 0.9.

Let me also think: GoToColor(green) when green doesn't exist - what does the robot do? It probably just wanders/explores. When green appears, it moves toward the green wall. If p=1.0 for C=7 transition, it stops immediately when it first sees green.

Actually p=0.9 vs p=1.0: p=0.9 means expected 1.1 steps to stop. p=1.0 means exactly 1 step. The difference is tiny per robot but with 20 robots over 600-800 post-switch steps, it could matter a little.

**Experiments:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. p=1.0 for green detection
3. Remove random stop entirely (n0=1, only green transition)
4. Very low random stop (p=0.0005)
5. Exploration base instead of GoToColor
6. Repulsion base instead of GoToColor
7. 3-state with exploration buffer
8. Different exploration rates
9. Higher p for green (already 0.9, try 0.95, 1.0)
10. GoAwayColor(green) before switch? No - robots need to be moving, direction doesn't matter much
11. Try with neighbor-based transitions in Stop state to prevent restart
12. p=1.0 AND remove random stop

## Reasoning

**Situation:** Champion unchanged at 5838.88. The round 3→4 didn't improve. Let me think more carefully.

**Deep analysis of the champion:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green=2) - moves toward green wall
- Before switch: no green exists, so robot wanders (good - keeps moving)
- After switch: green wall appears. Robot sees it → C=7 fires with p=0.9 → transitions to Stop
- State 1: Stop with tiny p=0.0005 restart

**Why is score ~5839?** With 20 robots over 1200 steps, worst case is 20×1200=24000. Score 5839 means roughly equivalent to 5839/20 ≈ 292 "bad" steps per robot.

**What might be going wrong:**
1. Before switch (~500 steps): robots should ALL be moving. But GoToColor(green) when no green exists - do robots actually move? They might be stationary if they can't see any green. This is a critical issue!
2. After switch: some robots may not see the green wall (blocked by other robots or walls), so they don't stop.

**GoToColor when no target visible:** The behavior likely makes the robot wander/explore when no target color is visible. This is the key assumption.

**Alternative approach:** Use Exploration as the base state (guaranteed movement) and transition to Stop on green detection.

**Why didn't Exploration variants improve?** Maybe GoToColor actually does move robots well, and the issue is elsewhere.

**New ideas to try:**
1. **Exploration + neighbor relay:** Robots that see green stop, others see stopped neighbors and also stop (cascade effect via neighbor count conditions)
2. **Higher p for green detection:** p=0.99 or p=1.0 with no random noise
3. **LED signaling:** Robots that stop emit a distinct color, others detect neighbor count changes
4. **3-state cascade:** Moving → (sees green OR many stopped neighbors) → Stop
5. **GoAwayColor as movement state:** robots actively flee from non-existent green, ensuring movement

Let me think about the cascade idea: if stopped robots cluster and others can detect "many neighbors" (because stopped robots cluster near green wall), that could help non-seeing robots stop too. But range is only 0.5m.

Actually, the neighbor cascade: robots near the green wall stop. Other robots moving toward green wall (GoToColor) will approach those stopped robots and see >threshold neighbors, then also stop. This is promising!

**Key new design:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green) 
- Transition 0→Stop: C=7 (sees green) p=0.9 OR C=3 (many neighbors, because stopped robots cluster) 
- State 1: Stop

Let me try transitions with both C=7 and C=3 (neighbors above threshold):

Also try: what if the stop state transitions BACK to GoToColor(green) when it sees green (to approach and re-stop)? No, that's counterproductive.

What about using C=4 (neighbors BELOW threshold) to restart from stop? If robot is isolated (away from clustered stopped robots), it should move. But we want all to stop.

Let me focus on the cascade approach and various p values:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation:** Champion barely improved (5838→5788). The core design is solid but we're stuck. Let me think more carefully about what's limiting performance.

**Theoretical minimum:** 
- Before switch (~500 steps avg): ALL 20 robots moving = 0 penalty. Perfect.
- After switch (~700 steps avg): ALL 20 robots stopped = 0 penalty. Perfect.
- But robots need time to detect green and stop. If detection takes T steps on average, penalty = 20×T.
- Score ~5789 / 20 = ~289 "bad" robot-steps total.

**Root causes of ~5789 score:**
1. **Before switch penalty**: GoToColor(green) when no green → robot behavior undefined. If it stops = bad. Need to verify it moves.
2. **After switch penalty**: Time to stop after green appears. With p=0.9 per step seeing green, expected ~1 step if robot can see green. But robots near the opposite wall may not see green wall!
3. **Camera range**: The camera has limited range. Robots far from green wall may not detect it for many steps.
4. **p=0.001 random stop**: Before switch, 0.001×500×20 = 10 bad robot-steps. Small but real.

**Key insight I've been missing:** Not all robots can see the green wall when it lights up! Robots far away or facing away may take many steps to detect it. GoToColor(green) helps because after switch, robots move TOWARD green wall, eventually seeing it and stopping. But the time to reach it matters.

**Better strategy idea:** Use LED signaling as a cascade:
- Robots that stop could change their LED color (already using yellow=4)
- Other robots detect stopped robots via neighbor count (many stopped robots near green wall = cluster)
- BUT: neighbor count only works within 0.5m range

**Alternative: Use C=7 (sees yellow LED) as cascade signal:**
- Stopped robots emit yellow (cle1=4)
- Moving robots: if they see yellow neighbors (C=7, l=4), they also stop
- This creates a cascade from robots near the wall outward

Wait - C=7 checks camera for colored objects, and robots emit LED colors that other robots can see with their cameras! This is the key cascade mechanism.

Let me redesign:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), LED=yellow
- Transition 0→Stop: sees green (C=7, l=2) p=0.9
- Transition 0→Stop: sees yellow robot (C=7, l=4) p=0.8 (cascade from stopped robots)
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow

This cascade means: robots near green wall stop first (emit yellow), robots behind them see yellow LEDs and also stop, creating a wave!

Also try: magenta LEDs for stopped state to distinguish.

Let me also try: State 0 = Exploration (guaranteed movement before switch).

**Plan:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2-5. Cascade designs with yellow LED detection
6-8. Exploration base with cascade
9-10. Pure GoToColor with p=1.0 tweaks
11-14. 3-state cascade machines

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Massive improvement!** Score dropped from 5788 to 298.52 - nearly 20x better! The cascade LED mechanism worked brilliantly.

**Champion analysis (score 298.52):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green=2), LED=off
  - → State 1 (Exploration): sees green (C=7, l=2) p=0.9
  - → State 1 (Exploration): sees yellow LED (C=7, l=4) p=0.9 (cascade from stopped robots!)
- State 1: Exploration(rwm=15), LED=off
  - → State 1 (self? No - destination among OTHER states): wait, n1x0 destination=1... 
  
Wait, let me re-read the destination indexing. With 3 states, leaving state 1, the other states are [0, 2]. So D=0 → state 0, D=1 → state 2.

- State 1: Exploration(rwm=15), LED=off
  - → State 2 (Stop): sees green p=1.0
  - → State 2 (Stop): sees yellow LED p=0.9
- State 2: Stop, LED=yellow
  - → State 0 (GoToColor): random p=0.0005

**This is genius!** The cascade works:
1. Robots near green wall: State 0 → State 1 (on seeing green or yellow)
2. Wait - State 1 is Exploration, not Stop. Then State 1 → State 2 (Stop) on seeing green or yellow.
3. State 2 (Stop) emits yellow LED.
4. Robots that see yellow LED (from stopped robots) also transition through State 1 → State 2.

Actually the path is: State 0 (GoToColor green) → sees green/yellow → State 1 (Exploration) → sees green/yellow → State 2 (Stop, yellow LED).

The two-step transition is interesting. Robots first enter exploration briefly, then stop. The yellow LED cascade means robots that can't see the green wall can still stop by seeing stopped yellow neighbors.

**Why score is so low (298.52):**
- Before switch: robots move (GoToColor + Exploration states)
- After switch: green appears, robots near it stop and emit yellow, cascade spreads rapidly
- Total bad steps ≈ 298.52/20 ≈ 15 bad steps per robot on average!

**What to try to improve further:**
1. Directly transition to Stop (skip Exploration intermediary) - might be faster
2. Higher p values (1.0) for the cascade transitions
3. Different LED colors for the cascade signal
4. Reduce the p=0.0005 restart or eliminate it
5. Add more cascade conditions (neighbor count + yellow LED)
6. Try making State 1 also GoToColor instead of Exploration (move toward green faster)
7. Make the cascade p=1.0 (instant stop on seeing yellow)

**Key question:** Why have State 1 (Exploration) as intermediary instead of going directly to Stop? Perhaps it helps ensure robots are actually moving before switch (if they enter state 1 briefly). Or maybe it's just how the cascade topology happened to work.

**Exploitation candidates:**
- p=1.0 for both cascade transitions in state 1
- p=1.0 for yellow detection in state 0
- Remove state 1, go directly to stop
- Add neighbor-count transitions as additional stop triggers
- Try cyan (l=6) instead of yellow for different range/visibility

**Exploration candidates:**
- 4-state version with more cascades
- Direct 2-state with yellow cascade
- Different movement behaviors in intermediate states

- State 1: Exploration(rwm=15), LED=off
  - → State 2: sees green (p=1.0)
  - → State 2: sees yellow LED (p=0.9)
- State 2: Stop, LED=yellow
  - → State 0: random (p=0.0001) [tiny restart]

**Score 258.88 with 20 robots:** ~259/20 ≈ 13 bad robot-steps total. Extremely good!

**What changed from round 6→7:** p=0.0001 restart (vs 0.0005) improved score from 298→258. So reducing restart probability helped.

**Further improvements to explore:**
1. **Eliminate restart entirely** - but must have at least 1 transition from state 2. Could use a condition that effectively never fires (very low p, or neighbor condition that's never met).
2. **p=1.0 for yellow detection in state 0** - currently 0.9, could be 1.0
3. **p=1.0 for all cascade transitions** - instant response
4. **Skip state 1** - go directly from state 0 to Stop (2-state with cascade)
5. **State 1 as GoToColor** instead of Exploration - move toward green faster after first detection
6. **Different restart condition** - e.g., neighbors below 1 (isolated robot) to restart, which would almost never trigger when clustered near wall
7. **Neighbor count as cascade** - stopped robots cluster, creating high neighbor counts
8. **Add neighbor-based transitions** to complement LED cascade

**Key insight:** The two-step cascade (GoToColor → Exploration → Stop) seems to help. Maybe the brief exploration step helps robots orient and see yellow LEDs from more stopped robots.

**The restart p=0.0001:** Over 700 post-switch steps with 20 robots: 0.0001 × 700 × 20 = 1.4 expected restarts. Each restart costs several steps. Reducing to 0.00005 or using a condition that never fires would help.

**Can we use a condition that never fires?** Neighbor count above 10 with very high steepness - almost impossible. Or C=3 with p=10, w=20 (needs >10 neighbors, impossible with 20 robots in 0.5m range).

**Best exploitation moves:**
1. p=0.0001 → 0.00005 or 0.00001 for restart
2. p=0.9 → 1.0 for yellow LED detection in state 0
3. Use impossible condition for "restart" (C=3, p=10, w=20)
4. Try p=1.0 for all cascade transitions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score 248.67):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green=2), LED=off
  - → State 1: sees green (p=1.0) 
  - → State 1: sees yellow LED (p=1.0) [cascade]
- State 1: Exploration(rwm=15), LED=off
  - → State 2: sees green (p=1.0)
  - → State 2: sees yellow LED (p=1.0)
- State 2: Stop, LED=yellow
  - → State 0: random (p=0.0001)

**Score 248.67 / 20 robots ≈ 12.4 bad steps per robot.** Almost perfect!

**What's causing the remaining ~248 bad steps?**
1. **Restart from Stop**: p=0.0001 × 700 steps × 20 robots ≈ 1.4 restarts. Each restart = multiple bad steps. This contributes ~5-10 to score.
2. **Before switch penalty**: Any robots that accidentally stop before switch. With p=1.0 for yellow cascade, if any robot emits yellow before switch... but state 2 LED=yellow only when stopped. The issue is the p=0.0001 restart - a restarted robot goes back to state 0 (moving) which is GOOD before switch but BAD if it happens after switch.
3. **Detection delay after switch**: Even with p=1.0, robots need to FACE the green wall and be within camera range. Robots on the opposite side of the arena may take several steps.

**Key remaining issue:** The restart p=0.0001 is causing some robots to leave Stop after switch. After restarting to State 0 (GoToColor green), they'll see green immediately (p=1.0) and go to State 1 (Exploration), then see yellow LEDs immediately (p=1.0) and return to Stop. So a restart costs about 2 steps minimum. With ~1.4 restarts × 2 steps = ~3 bad steps.

**Main remaining cost is detection latency** - the time from switch to when all robots have stopped.

**Further improvements:**
1. **Use impossible restart condition**: C=3 with threshold=10 and w=20 - essentially never fires (can't have >10 robots within 0.5m). This eliminates restart penalty entirely.
2. **Reduce rwm** in state 1 - faster turning might help robots orient toward green/yellow faster
3. **Add neighbor-count transitions** - robots near many stopped robots (clustered near green) could stop faster
4. **State 1 as GoToColor** instead of Exploration - actively move toward green to see it sooner
5. **4-state machine** with more cascade layers

**Best bet:** Replace restart condition (C=5, p=0.0001) with an impossible condition (C=3, p=10, w=20.0). This should eliminate ~3-10 points.

Also try: State 1 as GoToColor(green) instead of Exploration - this means after first detecting green/yellow, robot actively moves toward green wall, sees it immediately (p=1.0), stops.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Final round - must make it count!**

**Champion analysis (score 248.67):**
- State 0: GoToColor(green=2), LED=off → State 1 on seeing green(p=1) or yellow(p=1)
- State 1: Exploration(rwm=15), LED=off → State 2 on seeing green(p=1) or yellow(p=1)  
- State 2: Stop, LED=yellow → State 0 on random(p=0.0001)

**Remaining score sources (~248/20 ≈ 12.4 bad steps/robot):**
1. **Restart**: p=0.0001 × ~700 steps × 20 robots ≈ 1.4 restarts. Each restart takes robot through states 0→1→2 = ~2 steps moving after switch. Cost ≈ 3 bad steps total.
2. **Detection latency**: After switch, robots need to detect green/yellow. With p=1.0 transitions, as soon as they see it they stop. But if they're facing away or too far, they spend steps in states 0-1 (moving = BAD after switch). This is the dominant cost: ~245 bad steps.
3. **Before switch**: p=0.0001 random restart from Stop going back to moving = GOOD before switch. Very few robots will be in Stop before switch anyway.

**The impossible restart trick**: Replace C=5 p=0.0001 with C=3 p=10 w=20 (needs >10 robots within 0.5m, essentially impossible). This should save ~3 bad steps → score ~245-246.

**Detection latency reduction ideas:**
- State 0 as Exploration instead of GoToColor - might help robots cover more area and see green/yellow sooner
- Add MULTIPLE cascade colors - stopped robots emit yellow, but also detect magenta (another moving robot's LED indicating it saw green). No - complexity doesn't help if p=1 already.
- The two-step (0→1→2) means a robot needs TWO consecutive detections. After first detection (0→1), robot briefly explores. If it immediately sees yellow/green again (p=1.0 each step while it can see them), it stops in 1 more step. So the 2-step overhead is minimal (~1 step per robot = 20 bad steps). 

**Going to 1-step (direct 0→Stop)**:  With 2 states: GoToColor → Stop. This might be slightly faster but loses the exploration diversity.

**Key experiments for final round:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Impossible restart (C=3, p=10, w=20)
3. Very tiny restart p=1e-06
4. Direct 2-state with impossible restart
5. Different rwm values (1, 5, 50, 100) for state 1 - faster/slower turning
6. State 0 as Exploration instead of GoToColor
7. Add neighbor-count cascade to state 1
8. State 1 as GoToColor(green) - both states move toward green
9. 4-state with extra cascade layer
10. Add magenta as second cascade color
11. GoAwayColor(green) as state 0 to keep robots dispersed
12. State 0 with higher rwm to cover more area

The most promising: impossible restart + keep everything else. And trying different rwm values.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
